# BINV-16

<!-- ===== Page 004 ===== -->

Breast Cancer
Discussion
UPDATES
Updates in Version 4.2026 of the NCCN Guidelines for Breast Cancer include:
BINV-16
• HR-negative/HER2-positive,ypT1-4,N0 or ypN≥1 pathway revised: Fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki (category 1) for those with high risk of recurrence Or Ado-trastuzumab emtansine
(category 1) alone for 14 cycles. If ado-trastuzumab emtansine discontinued for toxicity, then complete (up to) 1 year of HER2-directed therapy with trastuzumab (category 1) ±
pertuzumab. If node positive at initial staging,  pertuzumabmm + trastuzumab (category 1); or fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki (category 1) for those with high risk of recurrence (see
BINV-M 4 of 10 for high-risk criteria)
BINV-M 4 of 10
• Preferred
Adjuvant setting, bullet 3, sub-bullet 1 revised: Fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki (category 1) for those with high risk of recurrence. defined as inoperable cancer (cT4, N0–3, M0 or
cT1–3, N2–3, M0) at presentation prior to neoadjuvant therapy or operable cancer (cT1–3, N0–1, M0) with axillary node–positive disease (ypN1–3) following preoperative therapy.
Preoperative setting: Fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki followed or preceded by THP (Paclitaxel + Trastuzumab + Pertuzumab) was added as a category 2A recommendation.
• Footnote k added: In the Destiny-Breast 05 trial, high risk was defined as inoperable cancer (cT4, N0–3, M0 or cT1–3, N2–3, M0) at presentation prior to neoadjuvant therapy or operable
cancer (cT1–3, N0–1, M0) with axillary node–positive disease (ypN1–3) following preoperative therapy.
• Footnote l added: The DESTINY-Breast11 study included patients with HER2-positive breast cancer with high-risk of recurrence including lymph node positive [N1-3] disease or with a
primary tumor stage T3-4 or inflammatory early breast cancer. Patients with a history of interstitial lung disease (ILD)/pneumonitis requiring treatment with steroids, ILD/pneumonitis at
screening, or ECOG performance status >1 were excluded from the study.
BINV-M 9 of 10
• Dosing added for Fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki followed by THP (Paclitaxel + Trastuzumab + Pertuzumab).
BINV-M 10 of 10
• Reference 42 added: Harbeck N, Modi S, Pusztai L, et al. Neoadjuvant trastuzumab deruxtecan alone or followed by paclitaxel, trastuzumab, and pertuzumab for high-risk HER2-positive
early breast cancer (DESTINY-Breast11): a randomised, open-label, multicentre, phase III trial. Ann Oncol 2026;37:166-179.
BINV-Q (3 of 15)
• First line
Datopotamab deruxtecan-dlnk changed to a category 1 recommendation.
• Erratum, footnote l revised: Sacituzumab govitecan-hziy may be considered for second or later line if not used in a previous line of therapy as second-line therapy.
BINV-Q (6 of 15)
• Vepdegestrant was added as a category 2A, other recommended option.
• Footnote bb added: For disease progression after at least one line of endocrine therapy + CDK 4/6 inhibitor in the metastatic setting.
BINV-Q (14 of 15)
• Dosing added for vepdegestrant.
BINV-Q (15 of 15)
• Reference 18 added: Campone M, De Laurentiis M, Jhaveri K, et al. Vepdegestrant, a PROTAC Estrogen Receptor Degrader, in Advanced Breast Cancer. N Engl J Med 2025;393:556-568.
MS-1
• The discussion relating to systemic therapy for metastatic breast cancer was updated.
Updates in Version 3.2026 of the NCCN Guidelines for Breast Cancer include:
Updates in Version 5.2026 of the NCCN Guidelines for Breast Cancer include:
BINV-P (3 of 3)
• Row 2 revised: If treatment was initiated with chemotherapy and Trastuzumab + Pertuzumab, and the chemotherapy was stopped, Trastuzumab + Pertuzumab Trastuzumab ±
Pertuzumab may be added to endocrine therapy (Aromatase inhibitor ± Palbociclib).
BINV-Q (3 of 15)
• First line, middle row revised: PD-L1 CPS <10 or not candidate for PD-1/PD-L1 inhibitor therapy and no germline BRCA1/2 PV.
BINV-Q (4 of 15)
• First line
Row 2 revised: Docetaxel + Trastuzumab + Pertuzumab (category 1, preferred) with maintenance Pertuzumab + Trastuzumab
If HR-positive: Endocrine therapy (Aromatase inhibitor ± Palbociclib) + Trastuzumab + Pertuzumab Trastuzumab ± Pertuzumab
Row 3 revised: Paclitaxel + Trastuzumab + Pertuzumab (preferred) with maintenance Pertuzumab + Trastuzumab
If HR-positive: Endocrine therapy (Aromatase inhibitor ± Palbociclib) + Trastuzumab + Pertuzumab Trastuzumab ± Pertuzumab
BINV-P (2 of 3)
• Second- and/or Subsequent-Line Therapy: Fulvestrant/Gedatolisib ± Palbociclib was added as a category 1, preferred option for tumors without a PIK3CA mutation following progression
on or after treatment with at least one line of endocrine therapy.

# BINV-1

<!-- ===== Page 005 ===== -->

Breast Cancer
Discussion
UPDATES
Updates in Version 1.2026 of the NCCN Guidelines for Breast Cancer include:
General
• References were updated throughout.
DCIS-1
• Workup, last bullet added: Assess for distress (Also for IBC-1)
• Footnote f added: Refer to the NCCN Guidelines for Distress Management (DIS-A) for the NCCN Distress Thermometer and Problem List, which includes social determinants of health. (Also for BINV-
1, PHYLL-1, PREG-1, PAGET-1, IBC-1)
• Footnote h revised: Surgical axillary staging should not be performed for preoperative (biopsy-determined) pure DCIS unless there is some clinical-radiographic-pathologic suggestion of invasion
or axillary metastasis. SLNB may be considered in 1) concern for persistently positive margins 2) excision in an anatomic location compromising the performance of a future SLNB procedure; or 3)
oncoplastic breast-conserving surgery (see BINV-D for considerations for SLNB).
• Footnote i revised: An SLNB may be considered in the setting of 1) mastectomy for DCIS., 2) excision in an anatomic location compromising the performance of a future SLNB procedure 3) oncoplastic
breast conserving surgery (see BINV-D for appropriate candidates for SLNB). Omission of SLNB in the setting of mastectomy may be considered in patients >50 years with non-palpable low-grade
ER-positive DCIS with no abnormal axillary findings and in patients in whom the primary reason for mastectomy is patient preference.
• Footnote n revised: Whole breast RT following BCS reduces ipsilateral breast tumor recurrence (IBTR) rates in for all DCIS by 50%–70%. Approximately half of the recurrences are invasive and half
are DCIS. The risk of local relapse increases with larger DCIS, palpable mass, grade III disease, margins ≤2 mm, ER-negative tumors, and age <50. Select patients with "low" risk DCIS may be
considered suitable for omission of RT (endocrine therapy alone, if all RTOG 9804 criteria are met: screen-detected, grade 1–2, size ≤2.5 cm, margins ≥3 mm). Omission of RT and treatment with
endocrine therapy alone may be considered if all RTOG 9804 criteria are met (screen-detected, grade 1–2, size ≤2.5 cm, margins ≥3 mm).
DCIS-2
• DCIS Postsurgical Treatment, header revised: Risk reduction therapy for ipsilateral breast following BCS
BINV-1
• Workup
Bullet 2, sub-bullet 2 revised: Breast ultrasound as necessary
Bullet 2, sub-sub-bullet added: Axillary ultrasound if considering omission of surgical nodal staging
• cT1-4 pathway, last column, bullet 1 revised: BCS ± surgical axillary staging + ± whole breast RT
• Footnote b added: Based on SOUND and INSEMA trials, patients >50 years and postmenopausal with cT1N0 (node negative by axillary ultrasound), with HR+/HER2-negative, grade 1–2 tumors who are agreeable to
receiving whole breast RT and endocrine therapy may be considered for omission of SLNB. Caution should be used in those with lobular histology. (Also for BINV-2)
BINV-2
• Header revised: Locoregional Treatment of cT1–3, cN0 or CN+, M0 Disease: Breast-Conserving Surgery (BCS) + ± Whole Breast RT
• Column 3 header revised: Pathologic Nodal Status
• Column 4, last pathway, option revised: Consider APBI/PBI in for appropriately selected patients who are low risk (category 1)
• Footnote r added: Based on data from the Europa trial (Meattini I, et al. Lancet 2025;26;37-50), older patients eligible for omission of RT may choose to receive RT alone instead of endocrine therapy with no adverse
effects on early breast cancer outcomes.
• Footnote removed: Sentinel node biopsy may be omitted based on the SSO Choosing Wisely recommendation in patients ≥70 years of age with HR+/HER2-negative and pT1, cN0 tumors (Hughes KS, et al. J Clin Oncol
2013;31:2382-2387. Kunkler IH, et al. N Engl J Med 2023:388:585-594).
BINV-3
• Column 3
Top pathway revised: Negative axillary nodes (pN0/pN0[i+]) and ≤pT2 (≤5 cm) and negative margins ≥1 mm
Bottom pathway revised: Any pT/pN with positive margins Margins positive
Pathway removed: Negative axillary nodes and ≤pT2 (≤5 cm) and negative margins but <1 mm.
• Footnote x revised: In the case of pN1mi (a micrometastasis [>0.2 to ≤2.0 mm]), and no axillary dissection, evaluate other patient risk factors when considering RT.
• Footnote y revised: PMRT may be considered for patients with multiple high-risk recurrence factors, including central/medial tumors or tumors ≥2 cm and at least one of the following: grade 3, ER-negative, young
age/longevity, or LVI.
• Footnote z added: In the case of pT3N0, high-risk features for considering PMRT include, but are not limited to, young age/longevity and/or LVI.
Updates in Version 2.2026 of the NCCN Guidelines for Breast Cancer include:
BINV-Q (3 of 15)
• First line
Sacituzumab govitecan-hziy + Pembrolizumab changed to a category 1 recommendation.
Datopotamab deruxtecan-dlnk changed to a preferred recommendation.

# BINV-5

<!-- ===== Page 006 ===== -->

Breast Cancer
Discussion
UPDATES
BINV-5
• Footnote mm revised: Updated results from the adjuvant APHINITY trial in HER2-positive early breast cancer, with a median follow-up of 8.4 11.3 years, have confirmed the benefit of adding Pertuzumab to
Trastuzumab plus chemotherapy in preventing recurrences. (Also for subsequent pages)
BINV-12
• Additional Workup
Bullet 1, sub-bullet revised: Consider Axillary ultrasound unless prior MRI demonstrated negative axillary nodes
• Footnote tt revised: If considering preoperative therapy, consider use of a gene expression assay during workup for postmenopausal patients with cN0, operable ER-positive, HER2-negative disease (Iwata
H, et al. Breast Cancer Res Treat 2019;173:123-133; Pease AM, et al. Ann Surg Oncol 2019;26:366-371). If considering preoperative therapy, consider use of a gene expression assay during workup for pre-
and postmenopausal patients with cN0 and postmenopausal patients with cN1, operable ER-positive, HER2-negative disease.
BINV-14
• Column 3
Top pathway revised: Adjuvant systemic therapy (complete planned regimen course if not completed preoperatively) + whole breast RT
◊Bullet 2 revised: cN1 and ypN0: Adjuvant whole breast RT ± boost to the tumor bed.; and strongly consider Comprehensive RNI is not routinely indicated with inclusion of any portion of the undissected
axilla at risk.
◊Bullet 3 added: cN2–3 and ypN0: Adjuvant whole breast RT ± boost to the tumor bed with comprehensive RNI including any portion of the axilla at risk.
◊Bullet 4 revised: Any ypN+: Adjuvant whole breast RT ± boost to the tumor bed ; and with comprehensive RNI with inclusion of including any portion of the undissected axilla at risk.
Bottom pathway revised: Adjuvant systemic therapy (complete planned regimen course if not completed preoperatively) + ± PMRT + RNI (after axilla adequately assessed by SLNB, TAD, or ALND)
◊Bullet 1 added: cT1–T3, cN0, and ypN0: PMRT + RNI is not routinely indicated.
◊Bullet 2 added: cT1–T3, cN1, and ypN0: PMRT + RNI is not routinely indicated.
◊Bullet 3 added: cN2–3 and ypN0; any ypN+; cT4 any N: PMRT + comprehensive RNI with inclusion of any portion of the axilla at risk.
◊Bottom pathway removed: Adjuvant systemic therapy, no PMRT
◊Sub-bullets removed:
– cN+ and ypN0: Strongly consider PMRT to the chest wall and comprehensive RNI with inclusion of any portion of the undissected axilla at risk.
– Any ypN+: PMRT is indicated to the chest wall + comprehensive RNI with inclusion of any portion of the undissected axilla at risk.
– cT4, any N: RT is indicated to the chest wall + comprehensive RNI with inclusion of any portion of the undissected axilla at risk.
– cT3, cN0, and ypN0: Consider PMRT to the chest wall + comprehensive RNI with inclusion of any portion of the undissected axilla at risk.
• Footnote xx revised: Complete planned systemic therapy regimen course, if not completed preoperatively. Among patients shown to be N+ prior to preoperative systemic therapy, SLNB has a >10% false-
negative rate when performed after preoperative systemic therapy, which can be improved by marking the most suspicious biopsied node prior to treatment, and at the time of surgery, using dual tracers,
obtaining ≥3 sentinel lymph nodes nodes and removal of the marked node (Targeted Axillary Dissection [TAD]).
• Footnote yy revised: Strongly consider RT boost for high-risk features (eg, high-grade disease, age <50 years central/medial tumors, tumors ≥2 cm, with ≥1 of the following: grade 3, ER-negative, LVI, high
residual cancer in the breast, or young age/longevity).
• Footnote zz revised: When considering PMRT + RNI for cN1 that has become ypN0 (after preoperative therapy), based on limited followup duration of NSABP B-51 study (Mamounas EP, et al. N Engl J Med
2025;392:2113-2124) and the inability to demonstrate long-term non-inferiority for this subgroup, a discussion of  potential risks of under-treatment should be weighed against the previously established long-
term survival benefits of PMRT+RNI, particularly for patients of younger age/longevity with ER-positive tumors, residual disease in the breast, and/or those with multiple traditional clinical pathologic high-risk
features. (Mamounas E, Bandos H, White J, et al. Loco-regional irradiation in patients with biopsy-proven axillary node Involvement at presentation who become pathologically node-negative after neoadjuvant
chemotherapy: Primary outcomes of NRG Oncology/NSABP B-51/RTOG 1304; Abstract GS02-07; SABCS 2023.)
BINV-16
• Top pathway, column 2 revised: Adjuvant endocrine therapy (category 1), consider ribociclib for eligible patients, see BINV-K 2 of 3 for eligibility criteria.
• ypT1-4,N0 or ypN≥1 pathway revised to include: "or fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki (category 1) for those with high risk of recurrence (see BINV-M 4 of 10 for high-risk criteria)"
BINV-17
• Breast imaging
Bullet 2 revised: Routine imaging of reconstructed breast is not indicated after mastectomy
• Lifestyle
Bullet 2 added: Menopausal hormone therapy is relatively contraindicated in survivors of hormonally mediated cancers; should be used with caution in those with increased genetic cancer risk. See NCCN
Guidelines for Survivorship.
Footnote ddd revised: Studies indicate that annual mammograms are the appropriate frequency for surveillance of patients with breast cancer who have had BCS and RT with no clear advantage to shorter
interval imaging. Patients should wait 6 to 12 months after the completion of RT to begin their annual mammogram surveillance. Suspicious findings on physical examination or surveillance imaging should
warrant immediate follow-up/workup might warrant a shorter interval between mammograms.
BINV-18
• Biomarker testing, sub-bullet 3 revised: Comprehensive germline and somatic profiling Multigene panel testing (MGPT) (somatic and germline) to identify candidates for targeted therapies
• Footnote kkk revised: In the setting of oligometastatic breast cancer, the currently available data do not support ablative metastasis-directed RT (ie, stereotactic body radiation therapy [SBRT]) for
extending overall survival (OS) or progression-free survival (PFS). In some cases, SBRT may be preferred over palliative RT to provide more durable local control and pain relief. Optimal management of
oligometastatic stage IV breast cancers remains uncertain. Routine utilization of locoregional treatment of the primary tumor and ablative treatment for metastatic sites have not been demonstrated to improve
survival outcomes in prospective trials, however, may reduce the risk of local progression and palliate symptoms. Compared with recurrent oligometastatic disease, patients with de novo oligometastatic
cancer have better outcomes and therapeutic decisions should be individualized and discussed in a multidisciplinary context. A large number of case series document long-term disease-free survival (DFS)
for patients with de novo oligometastatic breast cancer who received multimodality therapy leading to “no evidence of disease” (NED). For de novo oligometastatic disease with favorable features (HER2+ or
ER+, induced NED status after receipt of optimal multi-agent systemic regimens, limited sites of metastases, bone-only metastases, and young age/longevity), an aggressive multimodality approach involving
locoregional therapy of the primary tumor, ablative treatment to all sites of metastases, and "adjuvant-like" systemic therapy may offer improved long-term PFS and may be considered. (Also for BINV-21)
Updates in Version 1.2026 of the NCCN Guidelines for Breast Cancer include:

# BINV-19

<!-- ===== Page 007 ===== -->

Breast Cancer
Discussion
UPDATES
BINV-19
• Column 3
3rd row revised: Consider appropriate systemic therapy to best response if indicated then surgical resection if feasible + consider surgical axillary staging + PMRT
4th row revised: Consider appropriate systemic therapy to best response if indicated then surgical resection if feasible + consider surgical axillary staging if + repeat RT, if feasible and
indicated
• Column 4 revised: Consider appropriate adjuvant systemic therapy as clinically indicated and Supportive Care and see Principles of Survivorship (Also for BINV-20)
• Footnote removed: Consider systemic therapy to best response, then resect if possible. (Also for subsequent pages)
• Footnote removed: See the Discussion for additional information. (Also for subsequent pages)
BINV-20
• 1st row revised: Consider appropriate systemic therapy to best response if indicated, then surgical resection if feasible with ALND (for axillary recurrence) + RT if feasible
• 2nd row revised: Consider appropriate systemic therapy to best response if indicated, then surgical resection of gross disease ± ALND (for axillary recurrence) if feasible + RT if feasible
BINV-21
• Footnote removed: Routine surgical resection of the primary breast tumor is generally not indicated in the management of patients presenting with de novo stage IV (M1) disease.
Although there is no survival benefit, it may be considered for local control of the primary tumor. Discussion regarding management of the primary tumor in this setting must be
individualized.
BINV-22
• Section significantly revised.
BINV-23
• Terminology was changed from systemic therapy to cytotoxic therapy. (Also for subsequent pages)
• Column 2, revised: If not endocrine therapy refractory, consider: Alternate endocrine therapy ± targeted therapy (if premenopausal, consider ovarian
• ablation or suppression).
• Footnote ttt added: Cytotoxic therapy includes chemotherapy or antibody-drug conjugates (ADCs), or targeted therapy. (Also for subsequent pages)
BINV-A
• Section significantly revised.
BINV-B
• Clinical Indications and Applications
Bullet 1 revised: May be used for staging evaluation to define extent of cancer or presence of multifocal or multicentric cancer in the ipsilateral breast, or as screening of the
contralateral breast cancer at time of initial diagnosis (category 2B). There are no high-level data to demonstrate that the use of MRI to facilitate local therapy decision-making improves
local recurrence or survival Available data does not demonstrate that the use of preoperative MRI improves re-excision rates, local recurrence, or survival for DCIS or invasive breast
cancer in patients pursuing BCS.
BINV-C
• Right column
Bullet 2 revised: Alternative methods of birth control include non-hormonal–based intrauterine devices (IUDs), barrier methods, or, for patients with no intent of future pregnancies, tubal
ligation or vasectomy for the partner
Last bullet revised: Breastfeeding following after breast-conservation therapy cancer treatment is not contraindicated...
BINV-D
• Column 1, top pathway added: No palpable lymph node at diagnosis and negative axillary ultrasound
• SLN positive pathway
Bullet 1 revised: cT1–T2, T3h cN0 or pN1mic
Bullet 3 revised: pN1mic; 1–2 positive SLNs
• Footnote e added: Based on SOUND and INSEMA trials, patients >50 years and postmenopausal with cT1N0 (node negative by axillary ultrasound), with HR+/HER2-negative, grade 1–2
tumors who are agreeable to receiving whole breast RT and endocrine therapy may be considered for omission of SLNB. Caution should be used in those with lobular histology.
Updates in Version 1.2026 of the NCCN Guidelines for Breast Cancer include:

# BINV-E

<!-- ===== Page 008 ===== -->

Breast Cancer
Discussion
UPDATES
BINV-E
• Footnote added: Based on SOUND and INSEMA trials, patients >50 years and postmenopausal with cT1N0 (node negative by axillary ultrasound), with HR+/HER2-negative, grade 1–2
tumors who are agreeable to receiving whole breast RT and endocrine therapy may be considered for omission of SLNB. Caution should be used in those with lobular histology.
• Footnote removed: Sentinel node biopsy may be omitted based on the SSO Choosing Wisely recommendation in patients ≥70 years of age with HR+/HER2-negative and pT1, cN0
tumors (Hughes KS, et al. J Clin Oncol 2013;31:2382-2387. Kunkler IH, et al. N Engl J Med 2023:388:585-594).
• Footnote b removed: Based on emerging data from the SOUND trial, patients with HR+/HER2-negative tumors, pT1, N0 (node negative by axillary ultrasound), may be considered for
omission of SLN biopsy. Gentilini OD, et al. JAMA Oncol 2023;9:1557-1564.
BINV-G
• "For BCS" added to Absolute and Relative contraindications
• Absolute contraindications, bullet 5 revised: Patients diagnosed with gestational breast cancer (not receiving systemic chemotherapy) who cannot receive RT within 12–16 weeks; see
PREG-1
• Absolute contraindications, bullet 6 revised: Multicentric disease with any of the following criteria (mandate mastectomy)
BINV-H (5 of 7)
• Bullet 7revised: Skin- and nipple-sparing mastectomy should be performed by an experienced breast surgery team that works in a coordinated, multidisciplinary fashion to guide proper
patient selection for skin-sparing mastectomy, determine optimal sequencing of the reconstructive procedure(s) in relation to adjuvant therapies, and perform a resection that achieves
appropriate surgical margins.
• Last bullet added: Routine imaging of reconstructed breast is not indicated after mastectomy but diagnostic imaging may be required for new symptomatic concerns.
BINV-I (1 of 3)
• Optimizing Delivery of Individual Therapy,
Bullet 1, sub-bullet 5 last sentence added: Consider daily imaging for patients receiving prone breast radiotherapy.
• Whole Breast RT, bullet 2, sub-bullet 3 revised: Available data support the use of ultra-hypofractionated regimens whole breast RT of (28.5 Gy in 5 (once-a-week fractions) or 26 Gy in
5 daily fractions). The optimal way to incorporate a tumor bed boost and the long-term toxicity associated with its delivery in the ultrahypofractionated setting is yet to be established.
fractions may be considered for selected patients over 50 years following BCS with early-stage, node-negative disease, particularly those in whom a boost is not intended.
• Footnote c added: Daily image guidance in conjunction with regular 3D assessments (eg, cone beam CT, SGRT) for IMRT/VMAT techniques.
• Footnote removed: Alternatively, 26 Gy in 5 daily fractions over one week may be considered, though data beyond 5 years for local relapse or toxicity are not yet available for this
regimen. (Murray Brunt A, Haviland JS, Wheatley DA, et al. Hypofractionated breast radiotherapy for 1 week versus 3 weeks [FAST-Forward]: 5-year efficacy and late normal tissue
effects results from a multicentre, non-inferiority, randomised, phase 3 trial. Lancet 2020;395:1613-1626.)
• Footnote removed: Brunt AM, Haviland JS, Sydenham M, et al. Ten-year results of FAST: A randomized controlled trial of 5-fraction whole-breast radiotherapy for early breast cancer. J
Clin Oncol 2020;38:3261-3272.
BINV-I (2 of 3)
• PMRT (including breast reconstruction)
Last bullet added: For patients with positive surgical margins after neoadjuvant systemic therapy, re-excision to negative margins is preferred. If not feasible, then strongly consider
PMRT to chest wall ± comprehensive RNI (including any portion of the undissected axilla at risk).
Bullet removed: In the case of cT3N0, high-risk features for considering PMRT include, but are not limited to, young age and/or LVI.
• RNI
Bullet 3 added: RNI generally includes supraclavicular, infraclavicular, internal mammary and axillary nodes at risk. Coverage of the internal mammary nodes may be individually
determined based on tumor location (medial/central), tumor size, extent of nodal involvement, and dose to the OAR.
Bullet 4, sub-bullet 1 revised: RT doses to the regional nodes of 46–50 Gy (conventional fractionation) or 39–42.56 Gy (moderately fractionated) dosing schedules are similar to PMRT
and whole breast may be considered.
Footnote d added: Bolus should be used for patients with skin involvement, positive superficial margins, and those with dermal lymphatic invasion and/or extensive LVI.
Footnote e added: Preliminary data show similar normal tissue toxicity at 5 years with 26 Gy in 5 fractions, for patients receiving regional nodal irradiation without inclusion of internal
mammary nodes. Longer-term toxicity and efficacy data are not yet available, and limited data are available in the context of reconstruction. (UK FF Nodal Sub-study; Murray Brunt et
al, Rad Onc 2025).
BINV-I (3 of 3)
• Bullet 1 revised: The NCCN Panel endorses APBI/PBI for any patient without germline BRCA1/2 PVs who meets the criteria outlined in the 2023 2024 ASTRO guidelines. Patients aged
≥40 years are recommended for APBI/PBI if they have:

# BINV-M

<!-- ===== Page 009 ===== -->

Breast Cancer
Discussion
UPDATES
BINV-K (1 of 4)
• General Principles
Bullet 1, sub-bullet 1 revised: ER+ tumors: ER testing should be used to determine if a patient is a candidate for endocrine therapies. Patients with cancers with 1%–100% ER IHC
staining are considered ER+ and eligible for endocrine therapies, there are limited efficacy data on the subgroup of cancers with ER-low-positive (1%–10%) results. The ER-low-
positive group is heterogeneous with reported biologic behavior often similar to ER-negative cancers; thus, individualized consideration of risks versus benefits of endocrine therapy
and additional adjuvant therapies should be incorporated into decision-making. High ER expression is more likely to benefit from endocrine therapy; however, the potential benefit of
endocrine therapy should be discussed even with low ER expression.
Bullet 3, sub-bullet 1 revised: Level of ER expression: Low ER+ expression is less likely to benefit from endocrine therapy. High ER+ expression is more likely to benefit from endocrine
therapy; however, the potential benefit of endocrine therapy should be discussed even with low ER expression.
Footnotes from subsequent pages were added as bullets 4, 5, and 6.
BINV-K (2 of 4)
• Ovarian function assessment
Bullet 2 revised: Monitor estradiol and ± follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH)/luteinizing hormone (LH) levels:
Bullet 2, last sub-bullet revised: Prior to next dose of GNRH agonist, particularly in women under 45 years. Frequency of testing of estradiol and ± FSH/LH levels should be
individualized.
Last bullet added: If patient is not postmenopausal, sequential evaluation of hormonal status is recommended.
• Methods for OFS, bullet 1, sub-bullet 3 revised: Triptorelin: 3.75 mg IM every 4 weeks or 11.25 mg IM every 12 weeks.
• Header revised: Options for Initiation of OFS
Bullet 1 revised: Prior to or with start of chemotherapy (neoadjuvant preoperative or adjuvant)
• Duration of OFS
Bullet 1 revised: 5 years of OFS is optimal based on SOFT and TEXT trials. The 8-year DFS was 85.4% with OFS + Tamoxifen versus 80.2% with Tamoxifen alone. The 15-year DFS in
SOFT trial was 73.5% with OFS + exemestane versus 70.5% with OFS + tamoxifen versus 67.0% for tamoxifen alone. A minimum 2 years of OFS is encouraged. There are no data on
efficacy or safety to support prolonging of OFS >5 years.
BINV-K (3 of 4)
• Column 1 revised: Premenopausal at diagnosis (if postmenopausal at diagnosis) (Also for BINV-K 4)
• Column 4, header added: Extended Adjuvant Endocrine Therapy (Also for BINV-K 4)
BINV-M
• Section significantly revised.
BINV-N (3 of 5)
• Row 1, column 3, last sentence added: Non-randomized data suggest that patients with MammaPrint-high 2 (MP-H2) tumors have significantly worse outcomes when treated with taxane-
based regimens without anthracyclines compared to MP-H1 tumors, suggesting benefit of addition of anthracyclines to taxane-based chemotherapy in MP-H2 tumors.
BINV-N (4 of 5)
• Row 1, column 3, pN0 header added.
• Row 1, column 3, pN1 (1–3 positive nodes) added: For patients with HR-positive, HER2-negative, and pN1 tumors (1–3 positive lymph nodes), an optimized BCI model integrating tumor
size and grade is prognostic for distant recurrence at 10 years.
• Row 2, column 3
Bullet 1 revised: For patients with pT1 HR-positive, HER2-negative, and pN0 tumors or pN1 tumors (1–3 positive lymph nodes), a BCI (H/I) high (5.1–10) demonstrated significant rates
of late distant recurrence.
Bullet 2 revised: In secondary analyses of the MA.17, Trans-aTTom, and IDEAL trials, patients with HR-positive, pT1–T3, pN0 or pN1 tumors (1–3 positive lymph nodes) or pN+ who had
a BCI (H/I) high demonstrated significant improvements in DFS when adjuvant endocrine therapy was extended, compared to the control arm.
BINV-P (2 of 3)
• First-Line Therapy
Last bullet moved from Useful in Certain Circumstances and revised to: For HER2-negative tumors with PIK3CA activating mutations:
◊Sub-bullet added: Fulvestrant/Inavolisib/Palbociclib, see BINV-Q (6).
• Useful in Certain Circumstances for Second- and/or Subsequent-Line Therapy
Bullet 3 added: Imlunestrant + Abemaciclib
BINV-P (3 of 3)
• Row 1, sentence added: Aromatase inhibitor ± Palbociclib + Pertuzumab + Trastuzumab
• Footnote m added: The combination of imlunestrant + abemaciclib may be most effective in those with ESR1 mutations and no prior CDK4/6 inhibitor. The EMBER-3 trial (Jhaveri KL,
et al. N Engl J Med 2025;392:1189-1202) included patients with and without ESR1 mutations and compared the combination of imlunestrant + abemaciclib to imlunestrant alone versus
comparing with the standard treatment containing an endocrine therapy plus CDK4/6 inhibitor.

# NCCN

<!-- ===== Page 010 ===== -->

Breast Cancer
Discussion
UPDATES
Updates in Version 1.2026 of the NCCN Guidelines for Breast Cancer include:
BINV-Q (1 of 15)
• Header revised: Systemic Cytotoxic Therapy for Recurrent Unresectable (Local or Regional) or Stage IV (M1) Disease
• General considerations, bullets added:
The selection, dosing, and administration of anti-cancer agents and the management of associated toxicities are complex. Modifications of drug dose and schedule and initiation of
supportive care interventions are often necessary because of expected toxicities and individual patient variability, prior treatment, and comorbidity. The optimal delivery of anti-cancer
agents therefore requires a health care delivery team experienced in the use of anti-cancer agents and the management of associated toxicities in patients with cancer.
For information regarding DPYD testing, see the NCCN Guidelines for Colon Cancer.
Pembrolizumab and berahyaluronidase alfa-pmph subcutaneous injection may be substituted for IV pembrolizumab. Pembrolizumab and berahyaluronidase alfa-pmph has different
dosing and administration instructions compared to IV pembrolizumab.
Bullet 6, sub-bullet 2 revised: Consider cryotherapy of hands and feet to decrease the risk of peripheral neuropathy. Use of glove constriction may be considered as it helps with
reducing peripheral neuropathy.
• For Those Receiving HER2-Targeted Therapy
Bullet 2 revised: Pertuzumab, Trastuzumab, and hyaluronidase-zzxf subcutaneous injection may be substituted for IV Pertuzumab and Trastuzumab; however, the dosing and
administration instruction may differ. Pertuzumab, trastuzumab, and hyaluronidase-zzxf injection for subcutaneous use may be substituted anywhere that the combination of
intravenous pertuzumab and intravenous trastuzumab are given as part of systemic therapy. Pertuzumab, trastuzumab, and hyaluronidase-zzxf injection for subcutaneous use has
different dosing and administration instructions compared to the intravenous products.
BINV-Q (3 of 15)
• First line, row 1: Sacituzumab govitecan-hziy + Pembrolizumab was added as a category 2A, preferred recommendation.
• First line, row 2: Sacituzumab govitecan-hziy was added as a category 1, preferred recommendation.
• First line, row 2: Datopotamab deruxtecan-dlnk was added as a category 2A, other recommended recommendation.
• Footnote k added: See NCCN Guidelines for Hematopoietic Growth Factors.
BINV-Q (4 of 15)
• First line row 1, revised: Docetaxel + Pertuzumab + Trastuzumab (category 1, preferred) with maintenance Pertuzumab + Trastuzumab
If HR-positive: Aromatase inhibitor ± Palbociclib + Pertuzumab + Trastuzumab
• First line row 2, revised: Paclitaxel + Pertuzumab + Trastuzumab (preferred) with maintenance Pertuzumab + Trastuzumab
If HR-positive: Aromatase inhibitor ± Palbociclib + Pertuzumab + Trastuzumab
• First line row 3: Fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki + Pertuzumab was added as a category 2A, other recommended recommendation.
• Second/Third Line row combined.
• Footnote o added: Fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki is associated with ILD/pneumonitis. Regular monitoring for this serious side effect is recommended. For patients with a history of
ILD/pneumonitis, there are no data on managing safety or toxicity of this drug in a trial.
• Footnote q revised: Single-agent fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki may be considered in the first-line setting as an option for select patients (ie, those with rapid progression within 6
months of neoadjuvant or adjuvant therapy [12 months for Pertuzumab-containing regimens]). Fam-trastuzumab deruxtecan-nxki is associated with ILD/pneumonitis. Regular monitoring
for this serious side effect is recommended. For patients with a history of ILD/pneumonitis, there are no data on managing safety or toxicity of this drug in a trial.
• Footnote removed: Maintenance Pertuzumab/Trastuzumab after response (with concurrent endocrine therapy if ER+ and HER2+ metastatic breast cancer).
BINV-Q (6 of 15)
• Footnote v revised: Assess for ESR1 mutations at progression following prior lines of endocrine therapy (ctDNA preferred). If ESR1 negative and TF is low, consider reflex testing with
tissue-based NGS assay.
• Footnote w added: Tissue biopsy is preferred to ctDNA at detecting homozygous copy loss of PTEN or TMB.
• Footnote removed: The safety of Alpelisib in patients with Type 1 or uncontrolled Type 2 diabetes has not been established.
BINV-Q (7 of 15)
• Footnote removed: Tissue biopsy is more sensitive than ctDNA (liquid biopsy) at detecting homozygous copy loss of PTEN or TMB-H. (Also for BINV-Q 7)
BINV-Q (8 of 15)
• Row 1 column 4 revised: Tucatinib + Trastuzumab ± Fulvestrant was added as a category 2A, useful in certain circumstances recommendation.
BINV-Q (9 through 15 of 15)
• Dosing and references updated.

PHYLL-1
• Section significantly revised.
PAGET-1
• Top pathway, last column revised: Examination or BI-RADS 4 or 5 imaging positive for breast lesion. (Also for top pathway, column 1 on PAGET-2)
PAGET-2
• Pathways 2 and 3 revised to follow the same pathway as pathways 3 and 4.
• Column 4 revised: Consider breast MRI and tissue sampling, if indicated
• Column 5 revised: Central lumpectomy including NAC ± SLNB with whole breast RT
or
Total mastectomy including NAC ± SLNB with or without breast reconstruction
Central lumpectomy including NAC ± SLNB without RT (category 2B)
PREG-1
• Footnote a revised: CT scans and nuclear imaging (bone scan or PET/CT) and contrast-enhanced MRI are contraindicated during pregnancy.
• Footnote removed: Considerations and selection of optimal local therapy and systemic therapy are similar to that recommended in non–pregnancy-associated
breast cancer; see other sections of this guideline. However, the selection and timing of chemotherapy, endocrine therapy, and RT is different if pregnant. BCS
during the first trimester of pregnancy can be considered in those who will require adjuvant chemotherapy and can have adjuvant RT delayed until after delivery.
Chemotherapy should not be administered during the first trimester of pregnancy, and RT should not be administered during any trimester of pregnancy. Coordination
is recommended between the oncology and obstetrics teams to plan the optimal timing of systemic therapy administration during pregnancy. Most experience
with chemotherapy during pregnancy for breast cancer is from regimens that utilize various combinations of Cyclophosphamide, Doxorubicin, and Fluorouracil.
Considerations for postpartum chemotherapy are the same as for non–pregnancy-associated breast cancer.
• Footnote removed: Use of blue dye is contraindicated in pregnancy; radiolabeled sulfur colloid appears to be safe for SLNB in pregnancy. See Considerations for
Surgical Axillary Staging (BINV-D).
PREG-2
• New section added.
IBC-2
• Top pathway, treatment revised: Total mastectomy (skin-sparing and nipple-sparing mastectomy are contraindicated) + level l/ll axillary dissection + RT to chest wall
and comprehensive RNI with inclusion of any portion of the undissected axilla at risk ± delayed breast reconstruction

Ductal Carcinoma In Situ (DCIS)
Discussion
DCIS-1
a Encapsulated papillary carcinoma (EPC) without associated conventional
invasion is staged as pTis because behavior is similar to DCIS (per AJCC). SPC
should be specified as in situ or invasive based on WHO criteria but both forms
have favorable outcomes.
b The Panel endorses the College of American Pathologists Protocol for
pathology reporting for all invasive and noninvasive carcinomas of the breast.
http://www.cap.org.
c For risk criteria, see NCCN Guidelines for Genetic/Familial High-Risk
Assessment: Breast, Ovarian, Pancreatic, and Prostate.
d Principles of Dedicated Breast MRI Testing (BINV-B).
e The use of MRI has not been shown to increase likelihood of negative margins
or decrease conversion to mastectomy. Data to support improved long-term
outcomes are lacking.
f Refer to the NCCN Guidelines for Distress Management (DIS-A) for the NCCN
Distress Thermometer and Problem List, which includes social determinants of
health.
g Re-resection(s) may be performed in an effort to obtain negative margins in
patients desiring breast-conservation therapy. Patients in whom adequate
surgical margins cannot be achieved with BCS should undergo a total
mastectomy. For definition of adequate surgical margins, see Margin Status
Recommendations After BCS for Invasive Cancers and DCIS (BINV-F).

Postsurgical
Treatment (DCIS-2)
h Surgical axillary staging should not be performed for preoperative (biopsy-
determined) pure DCIS unless there is some clinical-radiographic-pathologic
suggestion of invasion or axillary metastasis. SLNB may be considered for 1)
concern of persistently positive margins 2) excision in an anatomic location
compromising the performance of a future SLNB procedure; or 3) oncoplastic
breast-conserving surgery (see BINV-D for considerations for SLNB).
i Omission of SLNB in the setting of mastectomy may be considered in patients >50
years with non-palpable low-grade ER-positive DCIS with no abnormal axillary
findings and in patients in whom the primary reason for mastectomy is patient
preference.
j Principles of Breast Reconstruction Following Surgery (BINV-H).
k Invasive disease at total mastectomy or re-excision should be managed as
clinical stage l or stage ll disease (BINV-1).
l Principles of Radiation Therapy (BINV-I).
m Contraindications to Breast-Conserving Approaches Requiring RT (BINV-G).
n Whole breast RT following BCS reduces ipsilateral breast tumor recurrence
(IBTR) rates for all DCIS by 50%–70%. Approximately half of the recurrences
are invasive and half are DCIS. The risk of local relapse increases with larger
DCIS, palpable mass, grade III disease, margins ≤2 mm, ER-negative tumors,
and age <50. Omission of RT and treatment with endocrine therapy alone may be
considered if all RTOG 9804 criteria are met (screen-detected, grade 1–2, size
≤2.5 cm, margins ≥3 mm).

# NCCN

<!-- ===== Page 013 ===== -->

Ductal Carcinoma In Situ (DCIS)
Discussion
DCIS-2
o CYP2D6 genotype testing is not recommended for patients considering tamoxifen.
p Available data suggest endocrine therapy provides risk reduction in the ipsilateral breast treated with breast conservation and in the contralateral breast in patients
with mastectomy or breast conservation with ER-positive primary tumors. Since a survival advantage has not been demonstrated, individual consideration of risks and
benefits is important.
q The use of a bisphosphonate (PO/IV) or denosumab is acceptable to maintain or improve bone mineral density (BMD) and reduce risk of fractures in postmenopausal
(natural or induced) patients receiving adjuvant aromatase inhibitor therapy. Optimal duration of either therapy has not been established. Benefits from duration beyond
3 years or optimal duration beyond 3 years is not known. Factors to consider for duration of anti-osteoporosis therapy include BMD, response to therapy, and risk
factors for continued bone loss or fracture. There are case reports of spontaneous fractures after denosumab discontinuation. Patients treated with a bisphosphonate
or denosumab should undergo a dental examination with preventive dentistry prior to the initiation of therapy, and should take supplemental calcium and vitamin D.
r Low-dose tamoxifen (5 mg per day or 10 mg every other day for 3–5 years) is an option if patient is symptomatic on the 20-mg dose or if patient is unwilling or
unable to take standard-dose 20 mg per day tamoxifen [10 mg every other day since 5-mg dose is not available in the United States]. This low dosage needs further
investigation in premenopausal individuals (DeCensi A, et al. J Clin Oncol 2019;37:1629-1637).
Risk reduction therapy for ipsilateral breast:
• Consider endocrine therapy for 5 years for patients with ER-
positive DCIS, if:
Treated with BCS and RTo (category 1)
Treated with excision alonep
• Endocrine therapyq:
Tamoxifeno,r for premenopausal patients
Tamoxifeno,r or aromatase inhibitor for postmenopausal
patients with some advantage for aromatase inhibitor therapy
in patients <60 years or with concerns for thromboembolism
Risk reduction therapy for contralateral breast:
• Counseling regarding risk reduction

• Interval history and physical exam every 6–12 mo for 5 y,
then annually
• First mammogram 6–12 mo, after end of RT, if given, or
after BCS (category 2B) if no RT is given and annually
thereafter

1) ≥70 y, HR+, HER2-negative, cN0, pT1 (≤2 cm)r,u

p Includes techniques such as local tissue rearrangement, local flaps, regional flaps, breast reduction, and
mastopexy to allow for greater volumes of resection while optimizing aesthetic outcomes in patients undergoing
BCS.
q Determined by surgery, clinical findings, or radiographic imaging.
r Based on data from the Europa trial (Meattini I, et al. Lancet 2025;26;37-50), older patients eligible for omission of
RT may choose to receive RT alone instead of endocrine therapy with no adverse effects on early breast cancer
outcomes.

and optional FDG-PET/CT.

aa According to WHO, carcinoma of no special type (NST) encompasses
multiple patterns including medullary pattern, cancers with neuroendocrine
expression, and other rare patterns.
bb There are rare subtypes of metaplastic carcinoma (low-grade
adenosquamous and low-grade fibromatosis-like carcinoma) that are
considered to have a favorable prognosis without adjuvant systemic therapies.
cc To be associated with favorable prognosis, the favorable histologic type
should not be high grade, should be pure (>90% as classified on the surgical
excision, not core biopsy alone), and should be HER2 negative. If atypical
pathologic or clinical features are present, consider treating as ductal/NST.

ee Although patients with cancers with 1%–100% ER immunohistochemistry (IHC)
staining are considered ER-positive and eligible for endocrine therapies, there are
more limited data on the subgroup of cancers with ER-low–positive (1%–10%)
results. The ER-low–positive group is heterogeneous with reported biologic
behavior often similar to ER-negative cancers; thus, individualized consideration
of risks versus benefits of endocrine therapy and additional adjuvant therapies
should be incorporated into decision-making. See Principles of Biomarker Testing

a For tools to aid optimal assessment and comprehensive care of older adults, see
NCCN Guidelines for Older Adult Oncology.
e Principles of Biomarker Testing (BINV-A).
k Special Considerations for Breast Cancer in Males (Sex Assigned at Birth) (BINV-J).
aa According to WHO, carcinoma of NST encompasses multiple patterns including
medullary pattern, cancers with neuroendocrine expression, and other rare patterns.
ee Although patients with cancers with 1%–100% ER IHC staining are considered
ER-positive and eligible for endocrine therapies, there are more limited data on the
subgroup of cancers with ER-low–positive (1%–10%) results. The ER-low–positive
group is heterogeneous with reported biologic behavior often similar to ER-negative
cancers; thus, individualized consideration of risks versus benefits of endocrine
therapy and additional adjuvant therapies should be incorporated into decision-
making. See Principles of Biomarker Testing (BINV-A).
gg Adjuvant Endocrine ± CDK/4/6 Inhibitor Therapy and Principles of Adjuvant
Endocrine Therapy (BINV-K).
hh Preoperative/Adjuvant Therapy Regimens (BINV-M).
ii The benefit of HER2-targeted therapy is uncertain in HER2-positive breast cancer
with pT1a–b, N0 tumors, a population not studied in randomized trials. The decision
for use of trastuzumab therapy in this cohort must balance with the known toxicities.
Adjuvant chemotherapya,hh with
trastuzumab (category 1) (± pertuzumab
for pT2–T3) and endocrine therapygg,kk
jj The absolute benefit of HER2-targeted systemic therapy is negligible in patients
with HR-positive cancers and tumor size bordering on T1mic (<1 mm), and
endocrine therapy remains a viable option for systemic treatment.
kk Consider adjuvant bisphosphonate therapy for risk reduction of distant
metastasis for 3–5 years in postmenopausal patients (natural or induced) with
high-risk node-negative or node-positive tumors.
ll Consider extended adjuvant neratinib following adjuvant trastuzumab-containing
therapy for patients with HR-positive, HER2-positive disease with a perceived
high risk of recurrence. The benefit or toxicities associated with extended
neratinib in patients who have received pertuzumab or ado-trastuzumab
emtansine is unknown.
mm Updated results from the adjuvant APHINITY trial in HER2-positive early
breast cancer, with a median follow-up of 11.3 years, have confirmed the
benefit of adding pertuzumab to trastuzumab plus chemotherapy in preventing
recurrences.

vv FDG-PET/CT is most beneficial and accurate for advanced disease (stage III) and invasive ductal (compared to lobular) histology, but may be useful in selected
circumstances of earlier stage disease (stage IIA disease: cT1cN1, cT2cN0) such as: equivocal CT+ bone scan results; suspicion of undetected nodal and/or
distant disease; and treatment response assessment. An FDG-PET/CT may be utilized as an adjunct to, or in lieu of, initial standard staging and may be performed
simultaneously with diagnostic CT. Conversely, a bone scan or sodium fluoride PET/CT may not be needed if an upfront FDG-PET/CT clearly indicates consistent
findings on both PET and CT components.

k Special Considerations for Breast Cancer in Males (Sex Assigned at Birth) (BINV-J).
p Includes techniques such as local tissue rearrangement, local flaps, regional flaps, breast reduction, and mastopexy to allow for greater volumes of resection while
optimizing aesthetic outcomes in patients undergoing BCS.
t Principles of Radiation Therapy (BINV-I).
w Principles of Breast Reconstruction Following Surgery (BINV-H).
ww The accurate assessment of in-breast tumor or regional lymph node response to preoperative systemic therapy is difficult, and should include physical examination
and performance of imaging studies (mammogram and/or breast ultrasound and/or breast MRI) that were abnormal at the time of initial tumor staging. Selection of
imaging methods prior to surgery should be determined by the multidisciplinary team. MRI is more accurate than mammography for assessing tumor response to
preoperative therapy.
xx Among patients shown to be N+ prior to preoperative systemic therapy, SLNB has a >10% false-negative rate when performed after preoperative systemic therapy,
which can be improved by marking the most suspicious biopsied node prior to treatment, and at the time of surgery, using dual tracers, obtaining ≥3 sentinel lymph
nodes nodes and removal of the marked node (Targeted Axillary Dissection [TAD]).
yy Strongly consider RT boost for high-risk features (eg, central/medial tumors, tumors ≥2 cm with ≥1 of the following: grade 3, ER-negative, LVI, high residual cancer in
the breast, or young age/longevity).
zz When considering PMRT + RNI for cN1 that has become ypN0 (after preoperative therapy), based on limited followup duration of NSABP B-51 study (Mamounas EP,
et al. N Engl J Med 2025;392:2113-2124) and the inability to demonstrate long-term non-inferiority for this subgroup, a discussion of  potential risks of under-treatment
should be weighed against the previously established long-term survival benefits of PMRT+RNI, particularly for patients of younger age/longevity with ER-positive
tumors, residual disease in the breast, and/or those with multiple traditional clinical pathologic high-risk features.

# BINV-17

<!-- ===== Page 030 ===== -->

Invasive Breast Cancer
Discussion
BINV-17
SURVEILLANCE/FOLLOW-UP
Recurrent
Disease
(BINV-18)
ddd Studies indicate that annual mammograms are the appropriate frequency for surveillance of patients with breast cancer who have had BCS and RT with no clear
advantage to shorter interval imaging. Patients should wait 6 to 12 months after the completion of RT to begin their annual mammogram surveillance. Suspicious
findings on physical examination or surveillance imaging should warrant immediate follow-up/workup.
eee The use of estrogen, progesterone, or selective ER modulators to treat osteoporosis or osteopenia in patients with breast cancer is discouraged. The use of a
bisphosphonate (oral/IV) or denosumab is acceptable to maintain or to improve BMD and reduce risk of fractures in postmenopausal (natural or induced) patients
receiving adjuvant aromatase inhibitor therapy. Optimal duration of either therapy has not been established. Benefits of duration beyond 3 years or optimal duration
beyond 3 years is not known. Factors to consider for duration of anti-osteoporosis therapy include BMD, response to therapy, and risk factors for continued bone loss
or fracture. There are case reports of spontaneous fractures after denosumab discontinuation. Patients treated with a bisphosphonate or denosumab should undergo
a dental examination with preventive dentistry prior to the initiation of therapy, and should take supplemental calcium and vitamin D.
Exam:
• History and physical exam 1–4 times per year as clinically
appropriate for 5 years, then annually
Genetic screening:
• Periodic screening for changes in family history and genetic
testing indications and referral to genetic counseling as
indicated, see NCCN Guidelines for Genetic/Familial High-
Risk Assessment: Breast, Ovarian, Pancreatic, and Prostate
Postsurgical management:
• Educate, monitor, and refer for lymphedema management,
see NCCN Guidelines for Survivorship: Lymphedema
Breast imaging:
• Mammography every 12 months, beginning 6 months or
more after completion of breast-conservation therapyddd
• Routine imaging of reconstructed breast is not indicated
after mastectomy
• For patients with germline mutations or family history of
breast cancer, please refer to NCCN Guidelines for Genetic/
Familial High-Risk Assessment: Breast, Ovarian, Pancreatic,
and Prostate
Fertility, birth control, and sexual health (see BINV-C)
Screening for metastases:
• In the absence of clinical signs and symptoms suggestive
of recurrent disease, there is no indication for laboratory or
imaging studies for metastases screening.
Post-treatment monitoring:
• Cardiotoxicity monitoring for patients who received left-
sided RT, anthracyclines, or HER2-targeted therapy. For
anthracycline-induced toxicity, see NCCN Guidelines for
Survivorship
• Provide guidance on risk of comorbidities
Endocrine therapy:
• Assess and encourage adherence to adjuvant endocrine
therapy
• Patients on tamoxifen:
Age-appropriate gynecologic screening
Routine annual pelvis ultrasound is not recommended
• Patients on an aromatase inhibitor or who experience ovarian
failure secondary to treatment should have monitoring of bone
health with a bone mineral density (BMD) determination at
baseline and periodically thereaftereee
Lifestyle:
• Evidence suggests that active lifestyle, healthy diet, limited
alcohol intake, and achieving and maintaining an ideal body
weight (20–25 body mass index [BMI]) may lead to optimal breast
cancer outcomes. See NCCN Guidelines for Breast Cancer Risk
Reduction
• Menopausal hormone therapy is relatively contraindicated in
survivors of hormonally mediated cancers; should be used with
caution in those with increased genetic cancer risk. See NCCN
Guidelines for Survivorship
Psychosocial support:
• Survivors are at elevated risk for fear of recurrence, distress,
anxiety, and depression that may persist for many years after
diagnosis. Periodic screening and referral to mental health
professionals if needed are recommended. See NCCN Guidelines
for Survivorship
Communication:
• Coordination of care between the primary care physician (PCP)
and specialists is encouraged. Additionally, a personalized
survivorship treatment plan including personalized treatment
summary of possible long-term toxicity and clear follow-up
recommendations is recommended. See NCCN Guidelines for
Survivorship
Engagement:
• Patients frequently require follow-up encouragement in order
to improve adherence to ongoing screening and medication
adherence. See NCCN Guidelines for Patients

# BINV-18

<!-- ===== Page 031 ===== -->

Invasive Breast Cancer
Discussion
BINV-18
a	For tools to aid optimal assessment and comprehensive care of older adults, see NCCN
Guidelines for Older Adult Oncology.
e Principles of Biomarker Testing (BINV-A).
f For risk criteria, see NCCN Guidelines for Genetic/Familial High-Risk Assessment: Breast,
Ovarian, Pancreatic, and Prostate.
h Refer to the NCCN Guidelines for Distress Management (DIS-A) for the NCCN Distress
Thermometer and Problem List, which includes social determinants of health.
fff For the treatment of brain metastases, see NCCN Guidelines for Central Nervous System
Cancers.
ggg False-negative ER and/or PR determinations occur, and there may be discordance
between the ER and/or PR determination between the primary and metastatic tumor(s).
Therefore, endocrine therapy with its low attendant toxicity may be considered in
patients with non-visceral or asymptomatic visceral tumors, especially in patients
with clinical characteristics predicting for an HR-positive tumor (eg, long disease-free
interval, limited sites of recurrence, indolent disease, older age).
hhh In clinical situations where a biopsy cannot safely be obtained but the clinical evidence
is strongly supportive of recurrence, treatment may commence based on the ER/PR/
HER2 status of the primary tumor. Since ER/PR and HER2 status can change with
treatment and metastatic progression, it may be appropriate to consider repeat testing
on new samples in these scenarios if management will change.
iii Tumor tissue or plasma-based circulating tumor DNA (ctDNA) assays may be used and
each of these have benefits and limitations for diagnosis and disease progression. Tissue-
based assays have greater sensitivity for some alterations, but ctDNA may reflect tumor
heterogeneity more accurately. If one specimen is negative for actionable biomarkers,
testing on the alternative specimen can be considered.
jjj See NCCN Guidelines for Palliative Care and NCCN Guidelines for Supportive Care.
kkk Optimal management of oligometastatic stage IV breast cancers remains uncertain.
Routine utilization of locoregional treatment of the primary tumor and ablative treatment for
metastatic sites have not been demonstrated to improve survival outcomes in prospective
trials, however, may reduce the risk of local progression and palliate symptoms. Compared
with recurrent oligometastatic disease, patients with de novo oligometastatic cancer
have better outcomes and therapeutic decisions should be individualized and discussed
in a multidisciplinary context. A large number of case series document long-term
disease-free survival (DFS) for patients with de novo oligometastatic breast cancer who
received multimodality therapy leading to “no evidence of disease” (NED). For de novo
oligometastatic disease with favorable features (HER2+ or ER+, induced NED status after
receipt of optimal multi-agent systemic regimens, limited sites of metastases, bone-only
metastases, and young age/longevity), an aggressive multimodality approach involving
locoregional therapy of the primary tumor, ablative treatment to all sites of metastases,
and "adjuvant-like" systemic therapy may offer improved long-term PFS and may be
considered.

lll Multidisciplinary approach is especially important in the management of breast cancer recurrence to consider all potential treatment options for optimal outcomes.
mmm In patients with a local breast recurrence after BCS who had a prior SLNB, a repeat SLNB may be considered although the accuracy of repeat SLNB is unproven.
After mastectomy, repeat SLNB may be considered although there are limited data in this setting. In patients who had a prior axillary lymph node dissection (ALND)
or mastectomy, repeat SLNB with lymphoscintigraphy may be considered rather than ALND (Poodt IGM, et al. Ann Surg Oncol 2019;26:2417-2427).
nnn Repeat BCS ± adjuvant APBI/PBI may be considered in selected patients who decline mastectomy and otherwise meet consensus criteria for radiotherapy omission
or APBI/PBI. There are limited data for a repeat BCS in this setting.
ooo The decision to use RT to treat locoregional recurrence must factor in any prior radiation to the area and the risk of late normal tissue toxicity from the sum of the
prior and planned radiation courses.

SYSTEMIC TREATMENT OF RECURRENT UNRESECTABLE (LOCAL OR REGIONAL) OR STAGE IV (M1) DISEASEk
e Principles of Biomarker Testing (BINV-A).
k Special Considerations for Breast Cancer in Males (Sex Assigned at Birth) (BINV-J).
ee Although patients with cancers with 1%–100% ER IHC staining are considered ER-positive and eligible for endocrine therapies, there are more limited data on the
subgroup of cancers with ER-low–positive (1%–10%) results. The ER-low–positive group is heterogeneous with reported biologic behavior often similar to ER-negative
cancers; thus, individualized consideration of risks versus benefits of endocrine therapy and additional adjuvant therapies should be incorporated into decision-making.
See Principles of Biomarker Testing (BINV-A).
kkk Optimal management of oligometastatic stage IV breast cancers remains uncertain. Routine utilization of locoregional treatment of the primary tumor and ablative
treatment for metastatic sites have not been demonstrated to improve survival outcomes in prospective trials, however, may reduce the risk of local progression and
palliate symptoms. Compared with recurrent oligometastatic disease, patients with de novo oligometastatic cancer have better outcomes and therapeutic decisions
should be individualized and discussed in a multidisciplinary context. A large number of case series document long-term DFS for patients with de novo oligometastatic
breast cancer who received multimodality therapy leading to NED. For de novo oligometastatic disease with favorable features (HER2+ or ER+, induced NED status
after receipt of optimal multi-agent systemic regimens, limited sites of metastases, bone-only metastases, and young age/longevity), an aggressive multimodality
approach involving locoregional therapy of the primary tumor, ablative treatment to all sites of metastases and "adjuvant-like" systemic therapy may offer improved
long-term PFS and may be considered.
ppp Denosumab, zoledronic acid, or pamidronate (all with calcium and vitamin D supplementation) should be given (category 1) in addition to systemic therapy or
endocrine therapy if bone metastasis is present, expected survival is ≥3 months, and renal function is adequate. Patients should undergo a dental examination with
preventive dentistry prior to initiation of this therapy. The optimal schedule for zoledronic acid is every 12 weeks.
qqq Baseline assessment of bone density is recommended for patients receiving an aromatase inhibitor who are at risk of osteoporosis (eg, age >65, family history,
chronic steroids).

# BINV-28

<!-- ===== Page 041 ===== -->

Invasive Breast Cancer
Discussion
BINV-28
General Principles
• The management unresectable, stage IV (M1) or metastatic disease requires a
multifaceted approach, encompassing treatment and a comprehensive survivorship
plan.
• For principles of monitoring metastatic disease, see BINV-R.
• Encourage patients to promptly report any new or worsening symptoms. A
comprehensive evaluation is essential, as these symptoms could indicate disease
progression, treatment effects, or other unrelated medical issues.
• Additional health monitoring, management, and immunizations should be performed
as appropriate to the prognosis under the care of a PCP.
• Patients are encouraged to maintain a therapeutic relationship with a PCP throughout
their lifetime.
Treatment Management and Supportive Care
• Regular follow-up discussions about goals of care and care coordination may be
necessary to improve both survival and quality of life. Engaging patients in these
discussions, including therapy cessation, ensures that care aligns with their values
and preferences.
• Surveillance and follow-up plans should be individualized for all patients, particularly
older patients, considering comorbidities, functional status, and life expectancy.
Personalization of care is key to managing the complexities of older adults or disease
in those with multiple medical comorbidities.
• Patients should receive multidisciplinary and interprofessional care that seeks to
prevent and/or manage morbidities associated with breast cancer or its treatment.
• Patients often experience multiple transitions between different care settings and
health care providers. Coordination between primary care providers and specialists is
essential to ensure continuity of care.
• Addressing all treatment options at each progression, including the option of
supportive care alone or hospice care, is appropriate in patients with stage IV
disease.
• Early involvement of a palliative care team should be considered to provide advance
care planning, goals of care discussions, care transitions, psychosocial support, and
management of cancer and therapy-related symptoms.
• Routine screening, evaluation, and assessment for supportive care interventions and
referrals are necessary. Some of the symptoms are:
Hormone-related symptoms (NCCN Guidelines for Survivorship)
Cognitive dysfunction (NCCN Guidelines for Survivorship)
Peripheral neuropathy
Cancer-related pain (NCCN Guidelines for Adult Cancer Pain)
Cancer-related fatigue (NCCN Guidelines for Cancer-Related Fatigue)
Cancer-related infections (NCCN Guidelines for Prevention and Treatment of
Cancer-Related Infections)
Chemotherapy-induced anemia, neutropenia, and thrombocytopenia (NCCN
Guidelines for Hematopoietic Growth Factors)
Immunotherapy-related toxicities (NCCN Guidelines for Management of Immune
Checkpoint Inhibitor-Related Toxicities)
PRINCIPLES OF SURVIVORSHIP FOR THOSE LIVING WITH ADVANCED OR METASTATIC BREAST CANCER
Cardiac monitoring for patients treated with anthracyclines or HER2-
targeted therapy (NCCN Guidelines for Survivorship)
Nausea/vomiting, anorexia, cachexia, diarrhea, dyspnea, malignant
wounds, sleep-wake disturbances (NCCN Guidelines for Antiemesis and
other NCCN Guidelines for Supportive Care)
Lifestyle, Wellness, and Psychosocial Support:
• Patients should be encouraged to maintain a physically active lifestyle and a
healthy diet whenever possible. See NCCN Guidelines for Survivorship.
• Consider appropriate cancer and preventive health screenings per patient
age and sex assigned-at-birth. These decisions should be individualized
according to the patients prognosis.
• Screen for anxiety, fear of progression, depression, and post-traumatic
stress disorder (PTSD) related to cancer and its treatment. See
NCCN Guidelines for Distress Management and NCCN Guidelines for
Survivorship.
• Social determinants of health monitoring and referrals
Provide resources and assistance through financial navigators, social
workers, or other specified personnel to address financial concerns,
employment issues, and family support. Ensuring access to these services
can alleviate significant stress for patients.
◊See NCCN Guidelines for Palliative Care: Social Support/Resource
Management.
◊Financial Advocacy Services Guidelines (Association of Cancer Care
Centers).
◊Financial Toxicity and Cancer Treatment (National Cancer Institute).
See NCCN Guidelines for Distress Management.
• Facilitate connections with consumer groups, support networks, and
organizations that advocate for accessible and equitable care. Engagement
with these groups can empower patients and provide additional support.
• For any patients with sexual health concerns or for fertility and birth control
in premenopausal patients, see BINV-C.
Communication
• Consistent and clear communication while acknowledging uncertainties of
disease trajectory and prognosis is crucial.
• Treatment goals, survivorship issues, and the specific roles of each provider
should be communicated to the patient.
Engagement
• See NCCN Resources for Patients and Caregivers and Guidelines for
Patients.
• Address long-term survivorship issues specific to younger patients, such as
psychosocial development and educational or vocational support. These
patients may face unique challenges that require targeted interventions.
• Living will/end-of-life planning should be discussed.

# BINV-A

<!-- ===== Page 042 ===== -->

Invasive Breast Cancer
Discussion
PRINCIPLES OF BIOMARKER TESTING
HER2 (ERBB2) TESTINGa
• HER2 testing should be performed on all new primary or newly metastatic breast cancers using methodology outlined in the ASCO/CAP HER2 testing
guideline.a
• A re-review of the pathology with consideration for repeat or consultative HER2 testing should be made if a grade 1 (any histologic type), pure mucinous,
pure tubular, or pure cribriform carcinoma tests HER2-positive.a
• After a negative HER2 test result on initial biopsy sample, consider retesting on subsequent surgical or other additional sample if the initial sample was
suboptimal (eg, minimal invasive cancer was present, cold ischemic time or fixation was suboptimal), testing error is expected, additional samples contain
higher grade morphologically distinct cancer from the biopsy, to rule out heterogeneity in a high-grade cancer, or if it will otherwise aid in clinical decision-
making.a
• HER2 immunohistochemistry (IHC) results with a score of 0 should include the specific staining pattern present (0/absent membrane staining or 0+/with
membrane staining) per current CAP Breast Cancer Biomarker Reporting Protocol.
• Pathologists should be encouraged to examine any IHC 0/no staining carefully for any staining at high power, since the distinction between HER2
(ERBB2) IHC 0/absent membrane staining, IHC 0+/with membrane staining (faint, partial membrane staining in ≤10%), IHC 1+, or 2+/ISH negative
results (on primary or metastatic samples) is currently clinically relevant since patients with metastatic disease may be eligible for treatment targeting
non-amplified levels of HER2 expression.
• Laboratory must participate in a quality assurance accreditation program for HER2 testing. Otherwise, tissue specimen should be sent to an accredited
laboratory for testing. Health care systems and providers must cooperate to ensure the highest quality testing.
a	 NCCN endorses the ASCO/CAP HER2 testing guideline. “Principles of HER2 Testing” modified with permission from Wolff AC, Somerfield MR, Dowsett M, et al. Human
epidermal growth factor receptor 2 testing in breast cancer. Arch Pathol Lab Med 2023;147:993-1000.
BINV-A
1 OF 4

ISH negative results (on primary or metastatic samples) is currently clinically relevant since patients with metastatic disease may be eligible for treatment targeting

c Additional less common staining patterns such as moderate to intense but incomplete membrane staining (basolateral staining often seen in micropapillary cancers)

d Readily appreciated using a low-power objective and observed within a homogeneous and contiguous population of invasive tumor cells.

# BINV-A

<!-- ===== Page 044 ===== -->

Invasive Breast Cancer
Discussion
PRINCIPLES OF BIOMARKER TESTING
HER2 (ERBB2) TESTING VALIDATED BY DUAL-PROBE ISH ASSAYa,e,f,g
For HER2 testing validated by immunohistochemistry (IHC) assay, see BINV-A 2 of 4.
a	NCCN endorses the ASCO/CAP HER2 testing guideline. “Principles of HER2 Testing”
modified with permission from Wolff AC, Somerfield MR, Dowsett M, et al. Human
epidermal growth factor receptor 2 testing in breast cancer. Arch Pathol Lab Med
2023;147:993-1000.
e	Laboratory must participate in a quality assurance accreditation program for HER2
testing. Otherwise, tissue specimen should be sent to an accredited laboratory for
testing. Health care systems and providers must cooperate to ensure the highest
quality testing.
f	Single-probe ISH assays are not preferentially recommended but, if used, cases
with average HER2 (ERBB2) copy number ≥4.0 and <6.0 signals/cell should
base final results on concurrent IHC and if 2+ reflexed to dual probe ISH testing.
g	Evidence from trastuzumab adjuvant trials show that HER2 (ERBB2) testing
by ISH or IHC have similar utility to predict clinical benefit from HER2-targeted
therapy.
h	For ISH Groups 2–4 final ISH results are based on review of concurrent IHC,
with recounting of the ISH test by a second reviewer if IHC is 2+ (per 2018
CAP/ASCO Update recommendations). Additional report comments are
recommended for negative final results in these ISH groups.

3 OF 4
HER2-Negative
• (Group 5) HER2 (ERBB2)/CEP17 ratio <2.0 AND average HER2 (ERBB2) copy number <4.0 signals/cell
HER2-Negativeh (Determined by concurrent IHC and ISH results)
• (Group 2) HER2 (ERBB2)/CEP17 ratio ≥2.0 AND average HER2 (ERBB2) copy number <4.0 signals/cell and concurrent IHC 0-1+ or 2+
• (Group 3) HER2 (ERBB2)/CEP17 ratio <2.0 AND average HER2 (ERBB2) copy number ≥6.0 signals/cell and concurrent IHC 0-1+
• (Group 4) HER2 (ERBB2)/CEP17 ratio <2.0 AND average HER2 (ERBB2) copy number ≥4.0 and <6.0 signals/cell and concurrent IHC 0-1+ or 2+
HER2-Positiveh (Determined by concurrent IHC and ISH results)
• (Group 2) HER2 (ERBB2)/CEP17 ratio ≥2.0 AND average HER2 (ERBB2) copy number <4.0 signals/cell and concurrent IHC 3+
• (Group 3) HER2 (ERBB2)/CEP17 ratio <2.0 AND average HER2 (ERBB2) copy number ≥6.0 signals/cell and concurrent IHC 2+ or 3+
• (Group 4) HER2 (ERBB2)/CEP17 ratio <2.0 AND average HER2 (ERBB2) copy number ≥4.0 and <6.0 signals/cell and concurrent IHC 3+
HER2-Positive
• (Group 1) HER2 (ERBB2)/CEP17 ratio ≥2.0 AND average HER2 (ERBB2) copy number ≥ 4.0 signals/cell

# BINV-A

<!-- ===== Page 045 ===== -->

Invasive Breast Cancer
Discussion
• HR testing (ER and PR) by IHC should be performed on any new primary or newly
metastatic breast cancer using methodology outlined in the latest ASCO/CAP HR
testing guideline.1 DCIS should be tested for ER (PR not required).
• ER testing should be used to determine if a patient is a candidate for endocrine
therapies.
Cancers with 1%–100% of cells positive for ER expression are considered
ER-positive. Patients with these results are considered eligible for endocrine
therapies (applies to DCIS and invasive cancers).
Invasive cancers with between 1%–10% ER positivity are considered ER-low–
positive. While the potential benefit of endocrine therapy is largest in patients
with the highest percentage of ER expression, the potential for some benefit
should also be discussed with patients with low ER expression.
Cancers with <1% staining are considered ER-negative. Patients with cancers
with these results have not been shown to benefit from endocrine therapies.
• Laboratories should have standard operating procedures to maximize accuracy
and reproducibility of results for cases with <10% ER staining or weak intensity
staining (to avoid false negatives). The status of controls should be reported for
cases with these results.

4 OF 4
1 Allison KH, Hammond MEH, Dowsett M, et al. Estrogen and progesterone receptor testing in breast cancer: ASCO/CAP guideline update. J Clin Oncol 2020;38:1346-
1366; Arch Pathol Lab Med 2020;144:545-563.
• PR testing by IHC on invasive cancers can aid in the prognostic classification
of cancers and serve as a control for possible false-negative ER results.
Patients with ER-negative, PR-positive cancers may be considered for
endocrine therapies, but the data on this group are noted to be limited. The
same overall interpretation principles apply but PR should be interpreted as
either positive (if 1%–100% of cells have nuclear staining) or negative (if <1%
or 0% of cells have nuclear staining).
• Interpretation of any ER result by pathology should include evaluation of the
concordance with the histologic findings of each case. Clinicians should be
aware of when results are unusual and work with pathologists to attempt to
resolve (eg, repeat testing, consultative review) or explain atypical reported
findings. See tables below.
